# A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence

Mingyang Liu    Gabriele Farina & Asuman Ozdaglar Affiliation: LIDS,
EECS Affiliation: Massachusetts Institute of Technology
Affiliation: Cambridge, MA 02139, USA
Email: [{liumy19,gfarina,asuman}@mit.edu](mailto:)

###### Abstract

Policy gradient methods have become a staple of any single-agent
reinforcement learning toolbox, due to their combination of desirable
properties: iterate convergence, efficient use of stochastic trajectory
feedback, and theoretically-sound avoidance of importance sampling
corrections. In multi-agent imperfect-information settings
(extensive-form games), however, it is still unknown whether the same
desiderata can be guaranteed while retaining theoretical guarantees.
Instead, sound methods for extensive-form games rely on approximating
_counterfactual_ values (as opposed to Q values), which are incompatible
with policy gradient methodologies. In this paper, we investigate
whether policy gradient can be safely used in two-player zero-sum
imperfect-information extensive-form games (EFGs). We establish positive
results, showing for the first time that a policy gradient method leads
to provable best-iterate convergence to a regularized Nash equilibrium
in self-play.

|     |
| --- |
|     |

## 1 Introduction

In recent years, deep reinforcement learning (DRL) has succeeded
tremendously in many applications with large and complex environments,
such as games ([Mnih et al., 2013](#bib.bib36); [Silver et al.,
2017](#bib.bib43)), autonomous driving ([Kiran et al.,
2021](#bib.bib24)), robotics ([Ibarz et al., 2021](#bib.bib23)), and
large language models (_e.g._ [Ouyang et al. (2022)](#bib.bib37) and
ChatGPT). Much of these successes are due to the applicability of
scalable algorithms, such as proximal policy optimization (PPO)
([Schulman et al., 2017](#bib.bib42)) and soft actor-critic (SAC)
([Haarnoja et al., 2018](#bib.bib20)). The success of these algorithms
hinges on a few critical properties—these algorithms (I) only require
value estimates obtained from repeated random rollouts which can be
implemented efficiently; (II) converge in iterates (as opposed to in
averages), removing the need for either training an average policy
approximator or storing snapshots of past policies; and (III) soundly
avoid importance sampling corrections, which can be detrimental in
practice as they often lead to outsized reward estimates.

However, DRL is not applicable in multi-agent imperfect-information
settings, such as Texas Hold’em poker, where they tend to end up trapped
in cycles without making progress ([Balduzzi et al., 2019](#bib.bib3)).
Constructing policy gradient algorithms that enjoy the same wide
applicability as their DRL counterparts and yet retain theoretical
guarantees in tabular settings even in imperfect-information games is a
challenging, open direction of research. Current sound algorithms for
competitive games typically see their scalability limited by two major
obstacles: their lack of last- (or even best-) iterate convergence, and
their reliance on counterfactual values. In what follows we illustrate
both of these issues separately.

Average vs iterate convergence. In the last decade, most scalable
techniques to solve Nash equilibrium strategies in two-player zero-sum
imperfect-information extensive-form games (EFGs) have been based on
Counterfactual Regret Minimization (CFR) ([Zinkevich et al.,
2007](#bib.bib51)) and its modern variants ([Tammelin et al.,
2015](#bib.bib48); [Farina et al., 2019a](#bib.bib11); [Brown and
Sandholm, 2019](#bib.bib5); [Farina et al., 2021a](#bib.bib14)). These
algorithms guarantee that their _average_ strategy converges to the set
of Nash equilibrium (NE) strategies. However, average-iterate
convergence is not desirable within the regime of deep learning, where
strategies are stored indirectly as a vector of neural network weights.
To represent and use the average strategy, some authors have resorted to
storing in memory multiple snapshots of the network ([Steinberger,
2019](#bib.bib46); [Steinberger et al., 2020](#bib.bib47)), sampling one
at random; this can quickly become expensive in large games ([Liu
et al., 2023](#bib.bib31))). Alternatively, some authors have
included—as part of their pipeline—training a second network whose goal
is approximating the average strategy ([Brown et al., 2019](#bib.bib6)).
This approach is also undesirable, as it incurs an additional error in
the approximation of the average strategy.

In light of the above discussion, a recent trend in the literature has
focused on algorithms that do not require taking averages of strategies
and instead converge to the set of Nash equilibrium strategies—in short,
exhibit _iterate convergence_. A distinction between two forms of
iterate convergence is often made: _best-iterate_ convergence, meaning
that at least one of the iterates produced by the algorithm is very
close to equilibrium, and _last-iterate_ convergence, meaning that the
last iterate produced is. While this line of work has produced a wealth
of algorithms with provable last-iterate convergence [Daskalakis and
Panageas (2019)](#bib.bib9); [Anagnostides et al. (2022)](#bib.bib1);
[Wei et al. (2021)](#bib.bib50); [Lee et al. (2021)](#bib.bib30); [Cen
et al. (2021)](#bib.bib8); [Liu et al. (2023)](#bib.bib31), a major
obstacle towards practical combination with function approximation has
been the reliance of these algorithms on _counterfactual values_, which
we discuss next.

Counterfactual values vs Q-values. Aside from the cycling effect, there
is another reason that reinforcement learning, commonly based on
Q-values in single-agent environments, is not applicable in EFGs, which
is the fundamental _state_ concept being replaced by the _information
set_. Players’ expected payoffs of taking action $`a`$ at an information
set $`s`$ need to consider the opponent’s reach probabilities to $`s`$,
captured in the notion of _counterfactual values_. These are essentially
Q-values multiplied by the probability of the opponent and environment
reaching $`s`$ from the game’s root when the opponent follows their
strategy.

The downsides of counterfactual values become apparent when turning the
attention onto _estimation_ of values. Q-values are extremely practical
to estimate by performing random rollouts. For counterfactual values,
several techniques have been proposed. A popular technique in this space
is using importance sampling to estimate the counterfactual
values ([Lanctot et al., 2009](#bib.bib28); [Farina et al.,
2020](#bib.bib13)). However, importance sampling is not suitable for DRL
in large-scale environments due to the high dispersion (_i.e._, range of
values) of the produced estimator—for instance, the estimated Q-value
can be as large as the game size ([Kozuno et al., 2021](#bib.bib25);
[Bai et al., 2022](#bib.bib2); [Fiegel et al., 2023](#bib.bib16)), thus
hindering the stable training of neural networks. Certain variance
reduction techniques, such as those by [Schmid et al.
(2019)](#bib.bib41), still suffer a large dispersion even though the
variance is reduced.

External Sampling vs Trajectory Rollouts. Algorithms that sidestep the
need for importance sampling, such as External Monte Carlo CFR ([Lanctot
et al., 2009](#bib.bib28)), do that at the expense of exploring about
square-root of the EFG size in every iteration to reduce the variance of
the estimator. This is in stark contrast with DRL, which simply samples
a batch of trajectories at every iteration, each with a size
proportional to the height of the tree, which is typically logarithmic
in the size of the game. In large games such as Stratego, where the size
of the game tree is approximately $`10^{535}`$ ([Perolat et al.,
2022](#bib.bib39)), external sampling needs to visit more than
$`10^{200}`$ infosets at each iteration while trajectory rollouts only
visits no more than $`4\cdot 10^{3}`$ infosets per iteration.

Contributions. Given the above discussion, a question is natural:

_Is it possible to design a theoretically sound policy gradient method
for solving two-player zero-sum extensive-form games that achieves the
desiderata
[(I)](#S1.I1.i1 "Item (I) ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence"), [(II)](#S1.I1.i2 "Item (II) ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence"), [(III)](#S1.I1.i3 "Item (III) ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
listed in the introduction?_

Such an algorithm would enable estimating values via rollouts, without
need of importance sampling, all while ensuring iterate convergence,
bringing EFG technology more in line with modern DRL technology. Our aim
in this paper is to show that a positive answer to the question is
possible.

In this work, we develop the first principled policy gradient approach
for solving imperfect information EFGs. Our approach builds on a
particular notion of Q-values for EFGs, called _trajectory Q-values_,
which admits efficient estimation through random rollouts without
importance sampling. Our algorithm also introduces a new regularizer for
EFGs, which we coin _bidilated_ regularizer. When paired with trajectory
Q-values, the bidilated regularizer enables iterate convergence with
both full-information and stochastic feedback obtained through the
sampled trajectory. To obtain the results, we devised a novel learning
rate schedule that increases with the depth of the game tree. It ensures
that the strategies of ancestors are changing slower than those of the
children, and are therefore stable when the children are updating their
payoff estimates.

The discussion about related work is postponed to
[Appendix A](#A1 "Appendix A Additional Related Work ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence"),
and summarized in
[Table 1](#S1.T1 "In 1 Introduction ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").

convergence guarantee, feedback type, and supporting sampling or not.
(last) and (best) denote last-iterate convergence and best-iterate

## 2 Preliminaries

For any vector $`\bm{x}`$, we use $`x_{i}`$ as element $`i`$ of vector
$`\bm{x}`$ and $`\left\|\bm{x}\right\|_{p}`$ as the $`p`$-norm. We let
$`\left\|\bm{x}\right\|`$ denote the Euclidean norm
$`\left\|\bm{x}\right\|_{2}`$. We use $`\Delta^{n}`$ to denote the
$`(n-1)`$-dimensional probability simplex
$`\left\{\bm{x}\in[0,1]^{n}\colon\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=1\right\}`$. We
also define the Bregman divergence
$`D_{\psi}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\coloneqq\psi(\bm{x})-\psi(\bm{y})-\left\langle\nabla\psi(\bm{y}),\bm{x}-\bm{y}\right\rangle`$
with respect to the $`c`$-strongly convex function $`\psi`$. The
$`c`$-strong convexity of $`\psi`$ implies the bound
$`D_{\psi}(\bm{x},\bm{y})\geq\frac{c}{2}\left\|\bm{x}-\bm{y}\right\|^{2}`$.
For any integer $`n\geq 0`$, we use
$`[n]\coloneqq\left\{1,2,\cdots,n-1,n\right\}`$. For any set
$`{\mathcal{S}}`$, we denote with $`|{\mathcal{S}}|`$ as its
cardinality.

Extensive-Form Games. EFGs are played on a rooted game tree. In this
paper we focus on two-player zero-sum EFGs; hence, each node (also known
as _history_) belongs to exactly one player out of the set
$`\left\{1,2\right\}\cup\left\{c\right\}`$. The special player $`c`$ is
called the _chance player_, and models stochastic events (for example: a
roll of the dice or dealing a card from a shuffled deck) sampled from a
known distribution. We use
$`\mathcal{H}_{1},\mathcal{H}_{2},\mathcal{H}_{c}`$ to denote the set of
nodes belonging to each of the players. Terminal nodes (nodes without
children) have an associated payoff for each player, _i.e._
$`\mathcal{U}_{1}(h),\mathcal{U}_{2}(h)`$ for player $`1,2`$
individually and $`\mathcal{U}_{1}(h)=-\mathcal{U}_{2}(h)`$ for any
terminal node $`h`$ since the game is zero-sum.

To model imperfect information, the set of nodes $`\mathcal{H}_{i}`$ of
each player $`i\in[2]`$ is partitioned into _information sets_ (or
_infosets_ for short) $`s_{1},s_{2},\cdots,s_{m}`$. Nodes in the same
infoset are indistinguishable for the acting player of that infoset. For
example, in poker player 1 cannot distinguish two nodes in the game tree
that only differ on the private cards of player 2, since player 1 does
not observe the hand of the opponent. We use
$`{\mathcal{S}}_{i}\coloneqq\left\{s_{1},s_{2},\cdots,s_{m}\right\}`$ to
denote the collection of all infosets of player $`i`$. Let
$`\mathcal{H}\coloneqq\mathcal{H}_{1}\cup\mathcal{H}_{2}`$ and
$`{\mathcal{S}}\coloneqq{\mathcal{S}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{S}}_{2}`$ be the
joint set of nodes and infosets of player $`1,2`$ for convenience.
Because nodes in the same infoset are indistinguishable from the acting
player, they must all have the same action set, which we denote with
$`\mathcal{A}_{s}`$ as the action set of infoset $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$.
Furthermore, $`p\colon{\mathcal{S}}\to\left\{1,2\right\}`$ denotes the
player that an infoset $`s`$ belongs to.

We make the assumption that each player remembers all their past
observations and actions; this assumption is standard and goes under the
name of _perfect recall_. A direct corollary of this assumption is that
nodes in the same infoset $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}`$ have the same past
observation along the path from the root to the node in the view of
player $`i`$. For any two nodes $`h,h^{\prime}\in\mathcal{H}`$, we write
$`h\sqsubseteq h^{\prime}`$ if $`h`$ is on the path from the root of the
game tree to $`h^{\prime}`$. Suppose $`h\in s`$ and for any
$`a\in\mathcal{A}_{s}`$, we write $`(h,a)\sqsubseteq h^{\prime}`$ if the
path from the root of the game tree to node $`h^{\prime}`$ includes the
edge corresponding to taking action $`a`$ at $`s`$. For any two infosets
$`s,s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}`$ that belong to the same player
$`i\in[2]`$, whenever there exist two nodes
$`h\in s,h^{\prime}\in s^{\prime}`$ such that
$`h\sqsubseteq h^{\prime}`$, we write $`s\sqsubseteq s^{\prime}`$.
Similarly, we write $`(s,a)\sqsubseteq s^{\prime}`$ for any
$`a\in\mathcal{A}_{s}`$ when there exist nodes
$`h\in s,h^{\prime}\in s^{\prime}`$ such that
$`(h,a)\sqsubseteq h^{\prime}`$. Moreover, we can define
$`(s,a)\sqsubseteq(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})`$ for
$`s,s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}`$ for some $`i\in[2]`$ and
$`a\in\mathcal{A}_{s},a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{s^{\prime}}`$.
Furthermore, for any player $`i\in[2]`$, we define the _parent sequence_
$`\sigma(s)`$ of an infoset $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}`$ as the last
infoset-action pair $`(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})`$, where
$`s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i},a^{\prime}\in\mathcal{A}_{s^{\prime}}`$,
encountered along the path from root to any nodes in $`s`$ (the choice
of node in $`s`$ is irrelevant and $`\sigma(s)`$ is either unique or
non-existing due to the perfect-recall assumption ). If there does not
exist such infoset-action pair, we let $`\sigma(s)=\emptyset`$. For any
node $`h\in\mathcal{H}`$, we define $`\sigma_{i}(h)`$ as the last
infoset-action pair $`(s,a)`$, where $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}`$,
encountered along the path from root to $`h`$. Finally, we define the
_depth_ $`\mathcal{D}(h)`$ of a node $`h\in\mathcal{H}`$ as the number
of actions (of all players) on the path from the root of the game tree
to $`h`$. The depth $`\mathcal{D}(s)`$ of an infoset
$`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}`$ is the maximum depth of any node $`h\in s`$.
Furthermore,
$`\mathcal{D}\coloneqq\max_{h\in\mathcal{H}}\mathcal{D}(h)`$ is the
depth of the game.

Strategies in EFGs. Since players cannot differentiate nodes in the same
infoset, their strategies must be the same at all of them. For player
$`i`$ and infoset $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}`$ (_i.e._ $`p(s)=i`$), we use
$`\pi_{i}(a{\,|\,}s)`$ to denote the probability of taking action
$`a\in\mathcal{A}_{s}`$ at any node in infoset $`s`$. We use
$`\bm{\pi}\coloneqq(\pi_{1},\pi_{2})`$ to denote the strategy profile.
For a player $`i`$, given an assignment of $`\pi_{i}(a{\,|\,}s)`$ for
each $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i},a\in\mathcal{A}_{s}`$, then we can
represent strategies for the EFG via their _sequence-form_
representation ([Von Stengel, 1996](#bib.bib49)). This is a mapping
$`\mu_{i}^{\pi_{i}}\colon\bigcup_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i},a\in\mathcal{A}_{s}}\left\{(s,a)\right\}\to[0,1]`$
associated with strategy $`\pi_{i}`$ for each player $`i\in[2]`$, where
$`\mu_{i}^{\pi_{i}}(s,a)\coloneqq\mu_{i}^{\pi_{i}}(\sigma(s))\cdot\pi_{i}(a{\,|\,}s)`$
for any $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i},a\in\mathcal{A}_{s}`$. Note that
$`\mu_{i}^{\pi_{i}}(\emptyset)=1`$. For each $`h\in\mathcal{H}`$,
according to the definition above, $`\mu_{i}^{\pi_{i}}(\sigma_{1}(h))`$
is equal to the product of the probability of all of Player $`i`$’s
actions from the root of the tree down to node $`h`$. We use
$`\mu_{c}(h)`$ to denote the probability of reaching $`h\in\mathcal{H}`$
contributed by the chance player. We assume $`\mu_{c}(h)>0`$ for any
$`h\in\mathcal{H}`$, since otherwise $`h`$ will never be reached and
thus can be removed from the game tree.

For simplicity, let $`\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}`$ to be a vector with index
$`(s,a)`$, where $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i},a\in\mathcal{A}_{s}`$, and
$`(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}})_{(s,a)}=\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}(s,a)`$. In this
representation, the expected utility for Player 1 is the bilinear
function $`(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}})^{\top}\bm{A}\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}`$ ¹¹ 1
$`\bm{A}\in[-1,1]^{\sum_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}}|\mathcal{A}_{s}|\times\sum_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}_{2}}|\mathcal{A}_{s^{\prime}}|}`$
is the utility matrix of the game. (the utility for Player 2 is
$`-(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}})^{\top}\bm{A}\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}`$ since the game is
zero-sum). We define the convex polytope of all valid sequence-form
strategies as $`\Pi_{1},\Pi_{2}`$ for player $`1,2`$ respectively, and
$`\Pi\coloneqq\Pi_{1}\times\Pi_{2}`$. For simplicity, let
$`\bm{\mu}^{\bm{\pi}}\coloneqq(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}},\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}})`$ as
the concatenation of the sequence-form strategy of both players.
Sometimes, we will omit the subscript of $`\mu`$ when it is clear from
the context, such as writing $`\mu^{\pi_{p(s)}}_{p(s)}(s,\cdot)`$ as
$`\mu^{\bm{\pi}}(s,\cdot)`$.

Counterfactual and Q-Values. In this section we recall some key notions
of values for EFGs. Our exposition is mostly intuitive to avoid
notational burden; all definitions can be found in
[Appendix B](#A2 "Appendix B Formal Definition of Trajectory Q-Value, Q-Value, and Counterfactual Value ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").
We start from introducing these values for _nodes_ and for Player 1 (the
definitions are symmetric for Player 2), and will later extend the
definition to infosets.

- •
  The _Q-value_ $`Q_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(h,a)`$ associated with strategies
  $`\pi_{1},\pi_{2}`$ for node-action pair $`(h,a)`$ is defined as
  Player 1’s expected utility in the subtree rooted at $`h`$, when
  Player 1 follows $`\pi_{1}`$ and Player 2 follows $`\pi_{2}`$ after
  first selecting $`a`$ as their first action.
- •
  The _counterfactual value_ $`\mathsf{CF}_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(h,a)`$ is
  defined as the product of the corresponding Q-value with the
  probability that Player 2 and the chance player reach $`h`$; in
  symbols,

  |     |     |     |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  |     |

  ````math
  \mathsf{CF}_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(h,a)\coloneqq\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}(\sigma_{2}(h))\cdot\mu_{c}(h)\cdot Q_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(h,a).
  ``` |  |
  ````

- •
  The _trajectory Q-value_
  $`\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(h,a)`$
  is defined as the product of the corresponding Q-value with the
  probability of the path of actions from the root of the game tree down
  to $`h`$. In symbols,

  |     |     |     |
  | --- | --- | --- |
  |     |

  ````math
  \mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(h,a)\coloneqq\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}(\sigma_{1}(h))\cdot\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}(\sigma_{2}(h))\cdot\mu_{c}(h)\cdot Q_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(h,a).
  ``` |  |
  ````

We now extend these definitions from nodes to _infosets_. Consider
infoset $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$ and action $`a\in\mathcal{A}_{s}`$. For
trajectory Q-value and counterfactual value we simply have
$`\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(s,a)\coloneqq\sum_{h\in s}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(h,a)`$
and
$`\mathsf{CF}_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(s,a)\coloneqq\sum_{h\in s}\mathsf{CF}_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(h,a)`$.
For Q-value,
$`Q_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(s,a)\coloneqq\mathbb{E}_{h\sim d(\cdot{\,|\,}s)}[Q_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(h,a)]`$,
where
$`d(h{\,|\,}s)\propto\mu_{c}(h)\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}(\sigma_{1}(h))\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}(\sigma_{2}(h))\propto\mu_{c}(h)\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}(\sigma_{2}(h))`$
²² 2 Because $`\sigma_{1}(h)=\sigma(s)`$ for any
$`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}`$ and $`h\in s`$ due to the perfect-recall
assumption. for any $`h\in s`$. All definitions are symmetric for Player 2.

#### Regret and Equilibrium.

When players $`1`$ and $`2`$ play according to strategies
$`\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}},\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}`$ respectively, the utility of
Player $`1`$ is $`(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}})^{\top}\bm{A}\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}`$
and that of Player $`2`$ is
$`-(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}})^{\top}\bm{A}\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}`$ since the game is
zero-sum. An $`\epsilon`$-approximate Nash equilibrium (NE) of the game
is then defined as follows.

###### Definition 2.1 ($`\epsilon`$-approximate Nash Equilibrium).

For compact convex set $`\mathcal{C}_{1},\mathcal{C}_{2}`$, a strategy
profile $`(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\in\mathcal{C}_{1}\times\mathcal{C}_{2}`$ is
an $`\epsilon`$-approximate NE if

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                               |     |       |
| --- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ----- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\max_{\widehat{\mu}_{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}}\widehat{\mu}_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\mu_{2}-\min_{\widehat{\mu}_{2}\in\mathcal{C}_{2}}\mu_{1}^{\top}\bm{A}\widehat{\mu}_{2}\leq\epsilon.`$ |     | (2.1) |

When $`\epsilon=0`$, we also simply call the strategy profile a Nash
equilibrium (NE).
[Equation 2.1](#S2.E1 "In Definition 2.1 (ϵ-approximate Nash Equilibrium). ‣ Regret and Equilibrium. ‣ 2 Preliminaries ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
is also called the _exploitability_ of the strategy profile
$`(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})`$.

Given a sequence of $`T`$ strategy pairs
$`(\mu_{1}^{(t)},\mu_{2}^{(t)})\in\mathcal{C}_{1}\times\mathcal{C}_{2}`$,
we can define the regret of Player $`1`$ as (Player $`2`$’s is
analogous),

|     |                                                                                                                                                                       |     |       |
| --- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ----- |
|     | $`\displaystyle R^{(T)}_{1}\coloneqq\max_{\widehat{\mu}_{1}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\widehat{\mu}_{1}-\mu_{1}^{(t)}\right)^{\top}\bm{A}\mu_{2}^{(t)}.`$ |     | (2.2) |

A folklore result establishes a direct connection between regret
minimization and approximate Nash equilibrium. Specifically, when the
players’ strategies incur regret $`R^{(T)}_{1}`$ and $`R^{(T)}_{2}`$
respectively, then the _average_ strategies of the players form an
$`\epsilon`$-approximate NE, where

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

       ``` math
       \epsilon=(R^{(T)}_{1}+R^{(T)}_{2})/T.
       ```                                    |     | (2.3) |

## 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR)

In this section, we propose our policy gradient algorithm for EFGs,
which we coin _Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR)_. In QFR, for
each player $`i\in[2]`$ and state $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}`$, we enforce
the strategy $`\pi_{i}^{(t)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s)`$ to explore with
probability $`\gamma_{s}`$ using the exploration strategy
$`\bm{\nu}_{s}`$, in order to ensure that each infoset will be reached
with a positive probability $`\gamma>0`$.

Then, we show that QFR converges in best iterate to the _regularized
Nash equilibrium_. Specifically, QFR will converge to the solution
$`\bm{\mu}^{(\tau,\gamma),*}=(\mu_{1}^{(\tau,\gamma),*},\mu_{2}^{(\tau,\gamma),*})`$
of the original bilinear minimax objective plus additional
regularization term
$`\psi_{\rm bi}^{\Pi_{1}}(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}},\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}})`$ and
$`\psi_{\rm bi}^{\Pi_{2}}(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}},\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}})`$, which
we call _bidilated regularizer_.
$`\psi_{\rm bi}^{\Pi_{1}}(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}},\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}})`$ is
strongly convex with respect to $`\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}`$ and convex with
respect to $`\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}`$. Conversely,
$`\psi_{\rm bi}^{\Pi_{2}}(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}},\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}})`$ is
strongly convex with respect to $`\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}`$ and convex with
respect to $`\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}`$. In contrast to the original bilinear
objective, optimizing the regularized objective will stabilize the
training process, and result in better convergence results. Formally,
the _regularized and perturbed_ (perturb refers to the exploration) game
is,

|                                                 |                                                                             |                  |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| ----------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ----- |
|                                                 | $`\displaystyle\max*{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu*{1}^{\pi*{1}}\in\Pi*{1}\colon\\ |
| \forall s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1},~\pi_{1}(\cdot{\, | \,}s)\in\Delta^{                                                            | \mathcal{A}\_{s} | }_{\gamma_{s},\bm{\nu}_{s}}\end{subarray}}\min_{\begin{subarray}{c}\mu*{2}^{\pi*{2}}\in\Pi\_{2}\colon\\                                                                                                                                     |
| \forall s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{2},~\pi_{2}(\cdot{\, | \,}s)\in\Delta^{                                                            | \mathcal{A}\_{s} | }_{\gamma_{s},\bm{\nu}_{s}}\end{subarray}}\left(\mu_{1}^{\pi*{1}}\right)^{\top}\bm{A}\mu*{2}^{\pi*{2}}-\tau\psi*{\rm bi}^{\Pi*{1}}(\mu*{1}^{\pi*{1}},\mu*{2}^{\pi*{2}})+\tau\psi*{\rm bi}^{\Pi*{2}}(\mu*{1}^{\pi*{1}},\mu*{2}^{\pi\_{2}})`$ |     | (3.1) |

where
$`\Delta^{|\mathcal{A}_{s}|}_{\gamma_{s},\bm{\nu}_{s}}\coloneqq\left\{\bm{u}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{A}_{s}|}\colon\forall a\in\mathcal{A}_{s},~~u_{a}\geq\gamma_{s}\nu_{s,a}\right\}`$,
and $`\tau\geq 0`$ controls the magnitude of the regularizer. Note that
the NE of the non-regularized game can be computed by annealing the
regularization coefficient $`\tau`$ by using a standard technique ([Liu
et al., 2023](#bib.bib31)).

To decompose the regularizer $`\psi_{\rm bi}^{\Pi_{i}}`$ to each infoset
for efficient update, we resort to the concept _dilated regularizer_
([Hoda et al., 2010](#bib.bib21)). Take Euclidean norm for example,
unlike naively choosing
$`\frac{1}{2}\left\|\mu_{i}^{\pi_{i}}\right\|^{2}`$ as the regularizer,
dilated regularizer weights the regularizer
$`\frac{1}{2}\left\|\pi_{i}(\cdot{\,|\,}s)\right\|^{2}`$ at each infoset
$`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}`$ by the reach probability of player $`i`$ to
$`s`$, _i.e._
$`\frac{1}{2}\sum_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}}\mu_{i}^{\pi_{i}}(\sigma(s))\left\|\pi_{i}(\cdot{\,|\,}s)\right\|^{2}`$.
It is shown in [Hoda et al. (2010)](#bib.bib21) that the dilated
regularizer is strongly convex with respect to $`\mu_{i}^{\pi_{i}}`$.
However, dilated regularizer $`\psi^{\Pi_{i}}`$ only weights the reach
probability of player $`i`$, neglecting that of the opponents, of which
the asymmetry causes importance sampling when estimating the regularizer
term in a rolling trajectory. Therefore, a natural solution is weighting
the strongly convex regularizer
$`\psi_{s}^{\Delta}\colon\Delta^{|\mathcal{A}_{s}|}\to\mathbb{R}`$ at
each infoset $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$ (typically it is Euclidean norm or
negative entropy) by the reach probability of all players. Formally, the
_bidilated_ regularizer
$`\psi^{\Pi_{1}}_{\rm bi}(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}},\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}})`$ of
player 1 is defined as (that of player 2 can be defined similarly),

|     |     |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |           |     |
| --- | --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------- | --- | ----------------------- |
|     |     | $`\displaystyle\psi^{\Pi*{1}}*{\rm bi}(\mu*{1}^{\pi*{1}},\mu*{2}^{\pi*{2}})\coloneqq\sum*{s\in{\mathcal{S}}*{1}}\!\mu*{1}^{\pi*{1}}(\sigma(s))\!\!\left(\sum*{h\in s}\mu*{c}(h)\mu*{2}^{\pi*{2}}(\sigma*{2}(h))\!\right)\!\psi*{s}^{\Delta}(\pi\_{1}(\cdot{\, | \,}s)),`$ |     | (Bidilated regularizer) |
|     |     | $`\displaystyle\psi^{\Pi*{1}}(\mu*{1}^{\pi*{1}})=\sum*{s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}}\mu_{1}^{\pi*{1}}(\sigma(s))\psi*{s}^{\Delta}(\pi\_{1}(\cdot{\,                                                                                                                  | \,}s))`$  |     | (Dilated regularizer)   |

The details can be referred to
[Appendix D](#A4 "Appendix D Bidilated Regularizer ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").
For notational simplicity, let
$`\psi^{\Pi}(\bm{\mu}^{\bm{\pi}})\colon\Pi\to\mathbb{R}=\psi^{\Pi_{1}}(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}})+\psi^{\Pi_{2}}(\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}})`$
and
$`\psi_{\rm bi}^{\Pi}(\bm{\mu}^{\bm{\pi}})\colon\Pi\to\mathbb{R}=\psi_{\rm bi}^{\Pi_{1}}(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}},\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}})+\psi_{\rm bi}^{\Pi_{2}}(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}},\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}})`$.
With all ingredients at hand, we can introduce the algorithm Q-Function
based Regret minimization (QFR). At each timestep $`t`$, QFR will sample
a trajectory according to the current strategy $`\bm{\pi}^{(t-1)}`$
(Line 3 of
[Algorithm 1](#alg1 "In 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")).
Then, the _trajectory Q-value_ will be estimated from the trajectory
(Line 10-12, $`\psi`$ estimates the trajectory Q-value contributed by
additional bidilated regularizer of the game, $`W`$ estimates the that
contributed by the reward of the original game). Lastly, all infosets
along the trajectory will be updated with a variant of Regularized
Optimistic Mirror Descent (Reg-OMD) proposed in [Liu et al.
(2023)](#bib.bib31) (Line 14). In Reg-OMD, at timestep $`t`$, the
strategy $`\bm{\pi}^{(t)}`$ serves as the prediction of
$`\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\pi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t+1)}`$.
Then in the next timestep $`t+1`$,
$`\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\pi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t)}`$
will be updated with the trajectory Q-value estimated at
$`\bm{\pi}^{(t)}`$. The pseudocode of QFR is proposed in
[Algorithm 1](#alg1 "In 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").

1: Initialize
$`\pi_{i}^{(1)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s),\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{i}^{(1)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s)`$
as uniform distribution over $`\Delta^{|\mathcal{A}_{s}|}`$ for any
$`i\in[2]`$ and $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{i}`$.

$`\displaystyle\widetilde{q}^{(t-1)}(s_{k},a)=\begin{cases}\left(W+\tau\psi\right)\nabla_{a}\log\pi_{p}^{(t-1)}(a{\,|\,}s_{k})&a=a_{k}\\
0&\text{Otherwise.}\end{cases}`$

   ![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iYWxnMS5sMTMucGljMSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAxIDEiIHdpZHRoPSIxIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIj48L2c+PC9zdmc+)![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iYWxnMS5sMTMucGljMiIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAxIDEiIHdpZHRoPSIxIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIj48L2c+PC9zdmc+)![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iYWxnMS5sMTMucGljMyIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAxIDEiIHdpZHRoPSIxIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIj48L2c+PC9zdmc+)![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iYWxnMS5sMTMucGljNCIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAxIDEiIHdpZHRoPSIxIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIj48L2c+PC9zdmc+)
Compute the estimated value function as
$`\displaystyle\widetilde{V}^{\pi_{p}}(s_{k})=\mathbb{E}_{a\sim\pi_{p}(\cdot{\,|\,}s_{k})}\left[\widetilde{q}^{(t-1)}(s_{k},a)\right]-\tau\psi_{s_{k}}^{\Delta}\left(\pi_{p}(\cdot{\,|\,}s_{k})\right).`$

Update
$`\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{p}^{(t-1)},\pi_{p}^{(t-1)}`$
according to

|     |                                                                                                   |                                                                |                       |                     |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------- | ------------------- | --------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------- | --- | ----- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t)}\_{p}(\cdot{\, | \,}s*{k})\leftarrow\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}*{\pi\_{p}(\cdot{\, | \,}s\_{k})\in\Delta^{ | \mathcal{A}_{s_{k}} | }_{\gamma_{s*{k}},\bm{\nu}*{s\_{k}}}}`$ | $`\displaystyle\widetilde{V}^{\pi*{p}}\left(s*{k}\right)-\frac{1}{\eta*{s*{k}}}D*{\psi*{s*{k}}^{\Delta}}(\pi*{p}(\cdot{\, | \,}s*{k}),\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t-1)}*{p}(\cdot{\, | \,}s\_{k}))`$  |     |       |
|     | $`\displaystyle\pi^{(t)}\_{p}(\cdot{\,                                                            | \,}s*{k})\leftarrow\mathop{\mathrm{argmax}}*{\pi\_{p}(\cdot{\, | \,}s\_{k})\in\Delta^{ | \mathcal{A}_{s_{k}} | }_{\gamma_{s*{k}},\bm{\nu}*{s\_{k}}}}`$ | $`\displaystyle\widetilde{V}^{\pi*{p}}\left(s*{k}\right)-\frac{1}{\eta*{s*{k}}}D*{\psi*{s*{k}}^{\Delta}}(\pi*{p}(\cdot{\, | \,}s*{k}),\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t)}*{p}(\cdot{\,   | \,}s\_{k})),`$ |     | (3.2) |

15:    where
$`\Delta^{|\mathcal{A}_{s}|}_{\gamma_{s},\bm{\nu}_{s}}\coloneqq\left\{\bm{u}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{A}_{s}|}\colon\forall a\in\mathcal{A}_{s},~~u_{a}\geq\gamma_{s}\nu_{s,a}\right\}`$

$`\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{p(s)}^{(t)}`$)
![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iYWxnMS5sMTYucGljNSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAxIDEiIHdpZHRoPSIxIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIj48L2c+PC9zdmc+)![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iYWxnMS5sMTYucGljNiIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAxIDEiIHdpZHRoPSIxIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIj48L2c+PC9zdmc+)![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iYWxnMS5sMTYucGljNyIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAxIDEiIHdpZHRoPSIxIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIj48L2c+PC9zdmc+)   ![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iYWxnMS5sMTYucGljOCIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAxIDEiIHdpZHRoPSIxIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIj48L2c+PC9zdmc+)  ![](data:image/svg+xml;base64,PHN2ZyBpZD0iYWxnMS5sMTYucGljOSIgY2xhc3M9Imx0eF9waWN0dXJlIiBoZWlnaHQ9IjEiIG92ZXJmbG93PSJ2aXNpYmxlIiB2ZXJzaW9uPSIxLjEiIHZpZXdib3g9IjAgMCAxIDEiIHdpZHRoPSIxIj48ZyB0cmFuc2Zvcm09InRyYW5zbGF0ZSgwLDEpIG1hdHJpeCgxIDAgMCAtMSAwIDApIj48L2c+PC9zdmc+)

Algorithm 1 Q-Function based Regret minimization (QFR)

We define the largest learning rate among all ancestor infosets of
$`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$ as
$`\eta_{s}^{\rm anc}\coloneqq\max_{i\in[2],h\in s}\max_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\sqsubseteq\sigma_{i}(h)}\eta_{s^{\prime}}`$
($`\eta_{s}`$ is the learning rate of infoset $`s`$), and we have the
following theorem that establishes the best-iterate convergence of
[Algorithm 1](#alg1 "In 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
to the NE
$`\bm{\mu}^{(\tau,\gamma),*}=(\mu_{1}^{(\tau,\gamma),*},\mu_{2}^{(\tau,\gamma),*})`$
of
[Equation 3.1](#S3.E1 "In 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
with high-probability.

###### Theorem 3.1 (Informal).

Consider
[Algorithm 1](#alg1 "In 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").
When $`\frac{\eta_{s}^{\rm anc}}{\eta_{s}}\leq\tau C_{s}^{\eta,T}`$,
where $`C_{s}^{\eta}`$ is a game-dependent constant, and $`\eta_{s}`$ is
smaller than a game dependent constant (formally defined in
[Appendix F](#A6 "Appendix F Proof of Theorem ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence"))
for any $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$, we have the following guarantee with
probability $`1-2\delta`$.

|     |                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |     |       |
| --- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | ----- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\sum_{t=2}^{T}D_{\psi^{\Pi}}(\bm{\mu}^{(\tau,\gamma),*},\bm{\mu}^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\pi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t)}})\leq\widetilde{O}\left(\max_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\eta_{s}T\right)+\widetilde{O}\left(\frac{1}{\min_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\eta_{s}}\right)+\widetilde{O}\left(\sqrt{T\log\frac{1}{\delta}}\right).`$ |     | (3.3) |

The $`\widetilde{O}`$ notion hides the logarithm of $`T`$. The proof and
the formal version are postponed to Appendix
[F](#A6 "Appendix F Proof of Theorem ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").
[Theorem 3.1](#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 3.1 (Informal). ‣ 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
gives a high-probability upper-bound on the cumulative Bregman
divergence. By letting $`\eta_{s}=\Theta(1/\sqrt{T})`$ for all infoset
$`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$, the right-hand-side of
([3.3](#S3.E3 "Equation 3.3 ‣ Theorem 3.1 (Informal). ‣ 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence"))
is bounded by $`\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{T})`$. Therefore, it implies that
$`\sum_{t=2}^{T}D_{\psi^{\Pi}}(\bm{\mu}^{(\tau,\gamma),*},\bm{\mu}^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\pi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t)}})`$
is upper-bounded by $`\widetilde{O}(\sqrt{T})`$ with probability
$`1-2\delta`$. Then, there must exist some $`t^{*}\in[T]`$ so that
$`D_{\psi^{\Pi}}(\bm{\mu}^{(\tau,\gamma),*},\bm{\mu}^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\pi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t^{*})}})\leq\widetilde{O}(1/\sqrt{T})`$,
because the minimum over
$`\left\{D_{\psi^{\Pi}}(\bm{\mu}^{(\tau,\gamma),*},\bm{\mu}^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\pi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t)}})\colon t=2,3,\cdots,T\right\}`$
must be bounded by the average, which is $`\widetilde{O}(1/\sqrt{T})`$.
Therefore, by computing the exploitability (the expected utility
confronting a best-responding opponent) routinely, we can find an
approximate NE ([Liu et al., 2023](#bib.bib31)) of the regularized game.
Moreover, according to Lemma [Liu et al. (2023, Lemma
D.1.)](#bib.bib31), the exploitability of the regularized NE will be
bounded by $`O(\tau)`$ in the original game so that our iterates will
also get a low exploitability in the original game by fixing $`\tau`$ to
be small or anneal it as [Liu et al. (2023)](#bib.bib31).

## 4 Analysis

In this section, we provide the proof sketch of
[Theorem 3.1](#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 3.1 (Informal). ‣ 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").
[Section 4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Preliminaries and Basic Properties ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
introduces some necessary notions and properties for the analysis.
[Section 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Convergence with Full Information Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
shows the convergence of QFR under full-information feedback (traversing
all infosets at each iteration), and
[Section 4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Convergence with Stochastic Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
generalizes to the stochastic setting in
[Section 3](#S3 "3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").

### 4.1 Preliminaries and Basic Properties

In order to keep the presentation modular between Q-values and
trajectory Q-values, we will assume that, at each iteration $`t`$, the
local strategy at each infoset will be updated by taking a step in the
direction of some generalized _value vector_
$`q^{(t)}(s,\cdot)\in\mathbb{R}^{|\mathcal{A}_{s}|}`$. For each
$`a\in\mathcal{A}_{s}`$, (we will use $`\mathsf{CF}^{(t)}_{1}(s,a)`$
instead of $`\mathsf{CF}_{1}^{\bm{\pi}^{(t)}}(s,a)`$ and
$`\mu_{i}^{(t)}`$ instead of $`\mu_{i}^{\pi_{i}^{(t)}}`$ as the
shorthand notion), the relationship of counterfactual values and the
feedback is,

|     |                                                                                                                                                                               |     |     |
| --- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{CF}^{(t)}_{1}(s,a)=\begin{cases}\sum_{h\in s}\mu*{c}(h)\mu*{2}^{(t)}(\sigma\_{2}(h))\cdot q^{(t)}(s,a)&q^{(t)}(s,\cdot)\text{ is Q-value}\\[5.69054pt] |

\frac{q^{(t)}(s,a)}{\mu\_{1}^{(t)}(\sigma(s))}&q^{(t)}(s,\cdot)\text{ is trajectory Q-value}\\[8.53581pt]
q^{(t)}(s,a)&q^{(t)}(s,\cdot)\text{ is counterfactual value}\end{cases}`$ | | (4.1) |

It is noteworthy that when sampling a trajectory from the root to a
terminal node, the utility will be a good estimator of trajectory
Q-value. Therefore, to estimate $`\mathsf{CF}^{(t)}_{1}(s,a)`$, we need
to divide the reaching probability $`\mu_{1}^{(t)}(\sigma(s))`$ of
$`s`$, which can be extremely small and thus induces a large variance.
In the following, we will write
$`\mathsf{CF}^{(t)}_{1}(s,a)=m^{(t)}_{s}q^{(t)}(s,a)`$ and
$`m^{(t)}_{s}`$ is different for different types of $`q^{(t)}(s,\cdot)`$
according to
[Equation 4.1](#S4.E1 "In 4.1 Preliminaries and Basic Properties ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").

Note that the value of $`m^{(t)}_{s}`$ in most cases depends on the
strategies that are produced by the algorithm; hence, there is some
circularity in the dependence between the properties satisfied by
$`m^{(t)}_{s}`$ and those satisfied by our algorithm. To break this
circularity, at the heart of our correctness proof we will verify and
leverage two key properties of the sequence of $`m^{(t)}_{s}`$ that
arises from using our algorithm: _boundedness_ and _stability_, as
detailed next.

###### Property 1 (Boundedness).

For any $`t\in[T]`$ and $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$, we have
$`m^{(t)}_{s}\in[M_{1},M_{2}]`$ where $`0<M_{1}\leq M_{2}<+\infty`$.

###### Property 2 (Stability).

For any $`t\in[T-1]`$ and $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$, we define the largest
learning rate among all ancestor infosets of $`s`$ as
$`\eta_{s}^{\rm anc}\coloneqq\max_{i\in[2],h\in s}\max_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\sqsubseteq\sigma_{i}(h)}\eta_{s^{\prime}}`$,
where $`\eta_{s^{\prime}}`$ is the learning rate of infoset
$`s^{\prime}`$, then

|     |     |                      |                                           |                                     |
| --- | --- | -------------------- | ----------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------- | --- | -------------------------- |
|     |     | $`\displaystyle\left | m^{(t+1)}_{s}-m^{(t)}_{s}\right           | \leq C*{s}^{-}\eta*{s}^{\rm anc}`$  |     | (Additive Stability)       |
|     |     | $`\displaystyle\left | \frac{m^{(t+1)}_{s}}{m^{(t)}_{s}}-1\right | \leq C*{s}^{/}\eta*{s}^{\rm anc}.`$ |     | (Multiplicative Stability) |

Property [1](#Thmproperty1 "Property 1 (Boundedness). ‣ 4.1 Preliminaries and Basic Properties ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
will be satisfied by enforcing
$`\pi^{(t)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s)\succeq\gamma_{s}\bm{\nu}_{s}`$ for every
$`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$, where $`\gamma_{s}\in(0,1]`$ and
$`\bm{\nu}_{s}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{A}_{s}|}`$ are specified in
[Section G.2](#A7.SS2 "G.2 Bounding 𝑀_1,𝑀_2 ‣ Appendix G Auxiliary Lemmas ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").
The proof that our algorithm produces iterates that satisfy Property
[2](#Thmproperty2 "Property 2 (Stability). ‣ 4.1 Preliminaries and Basic Properties ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
can be found in
[Appendix C](#A3 "Appendix C Stability of Trajectory Q-value and Q-Value ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").

### 4.2 Convergence with Full Information Feedback

QFR runs a variant of Regularized Optimistic Mirror Descent (Reg-OMD)
([Liu et al., 2023](#bib.bib31)) algorithm to update the strategy in
each infoset. For notational simplicity, we define the reach probability
of opponents to an infoset $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$ as
$`\mu_{-p(s)}^{(t)}(s)\coloneqq\sum_{h\in s}\mu_{c}(h)\mu_{3-p(s)}^{(t)}(\sigma_{3-p(s)}(h))`$.
The update rule is

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\begin{split}\pi^{(t)}_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s)\!=\!\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\pi_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s)\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{A}_{s}|}_{\gamma_{s},\bm{\nu}_{s}}}&\left\langle\pi_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s),-q^{(t-1)}(s,\cdot)\right\rangle+\frac{\tau\mu_{-p(s)}^{(t-1)}(s)}{m^{(t-1)}_{s}}\psi_{s}^{\Delta}(\pi_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s))\\
&+\frac{1}{\eta_{s}}D_{\psi_{s}^{\Delta}}(\pi_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s),\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t)}_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s))\\
\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t+1)}_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s)\!=\!\mathop{\mathrm{argmin}}_{\pi_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s)\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{A}_{s}|}_{\gamma_{s},\bm{\nu}_{s}}}&\left\langle\pi_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s),-q^{(t)}(s,\cdot)\right\rangle+\frac{\tau\mu_{-p(s)}^{(t)}(s)}{m^{(t)}_{s}}\psi_{s}^{\Delta}(\pi_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s))\\
&+\frac{1}{\eta_{s}}D_{\psi_{s}^{\Delta}}(\pi_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s),\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t)}_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s))\\
\end{split}
``` |  | (4.2) |

where
$`\Delta^{|\mathcal{A}_{s}|}_{\gamma_{s},\bm{\nu}_{s}}\coloneqq\left\{\bm{u}\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{A}_{s}|}\colon\forall a\in\mathcal{A}_{s},~~u_{a}\geq\gamma_{s}\nu_{s,a}\right\}`$
and $`\psi_{s}^{\Delta}`$ is the regularizer chosen for infoset $`s`$.
Here $`q^{(t)}(s,\cdot)`$ can be the trajectory Q-value, Q-value, or
counterfactual value associated with $`\bm{\pi}^{(t)}`$. When
$`q^{(t)}(s,\cdot)`$ is the trajectory Q-value,
([4.2](#S4.E2 "Equation 4.2 ‣ 4.2 Convergence with Full Information Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence"))
is the full-information version of
([3.2](#S3.E2 "Equation 3.2In Algorithm 1 ‣ 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")).

By analyzing the update rule
([4.2](#S4.E2 "Equation 4.2 ‣ 4.2 Convergence with Full Information Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")),
we can get the following inequality. For any $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$ and
strategy
$`\pi_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s)\in\Delta^{|\mathcal{A}_{s}|}_{\gamma_{s},\bm{\nu}_{s}}`$,
we have

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\tau\mu_{-p(s)}^{(t)}(s)\psi_{s}^{\Delta}(\pi^{(t)}_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s))-\tau\mu_{-p(s)}^{(t)}(s)\psi_{s}^{\Delta}(\pi_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s))+m^{(t)}_{s}\langle-q^{(t)}(s,\cdot),\pi^{(t)}_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s)-\pi_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s)\rangle\right)`$ |  |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\leq`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{t=2}^{T}\underbrace{\left(\frac{m^{(t)}_{s}-m^{(t-1)}_{s}}{\eta_{s}}-\tau\mu^{(t-1)}_{-p(s)}(s)\right)}_{\hbox to7.56pt{\vbox to7.56pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 3.77788pt\lower-3.77788pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 4.95 0 C 4.95 2.73 2.73 4.95 0 4.95 C -2.73 4.95 -4.95 2.73 -4.95 0 C -4.95 -2.73 -2.73 -4.95 0 -4.95 C 2.73 -4.95 4.95 -2.73 4.95 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-1.99306pt}{-2.25555pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -2.76 -3.12)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}D_{\psi_{s}^{\Delta}}(\pi_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s),\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t)}_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s))+O(\eta_{s}T)+O\left(\frac{1}{\eta_{s}}\right).`$ |  | (4.3) |

Then, since
$`\left|m^{(t)}_{s}-m^{(t-1)}_{s}\right|\leq O(\eta_{s}^{\rm anc})`$,
$`\hbox to9.67pt{\vbox to9.67pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.83623pt\lower-4.83623pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.42 0 C 6.42 3.54 3.54 6.42 0 6.42 C -3.54 6.42 -6.42 3.54 -6.42 0 C -6.42 -3.54 -3.54 -6.42 0 -6.42 C 3.54 -6.42 6.42 -3.54 6.42 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.5pt}{-3.22221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform=matrix(1.0 0.0 0.0 1.0 -3.46 -4.46)} \pgfsys@hbox{61}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}\leq-\frac{\tau}{2}\mu^{(t-1)}_{-p(s)}(s)\leq-\frac{\tau\gamma}{2}\sum_{h\in s}\mu_{c}(h)`$
when $`\eta_{s}^{\rm anc}`$ is smaller then $`\eta_{s}`$ by a small
enough constant (please refer to Appendix
[E](#A5 "Appendix E Proof of Theorem ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
for details). Then, by applying the generalized regret decomposition
lemma ([Liu et al., 2023](#bib.bib31)) (details can be found in
[Lemma E.3](#A5.Thmtheorem3 "Lemma E.3 (Lemma 5.1 in [ ] ). ‣ Proof Sketch. ‣ Appendix E Proof of Theorem ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence"))
to
[Equation 4.3](#S4.E3 "In 4.2 Convergence with Full Information Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence"),
the difference of $`\pi_{p(s)}^{(t)}`$ and $`\pi_{p(s)}`$ in an infoset
$`s`$ can be extended to the difference of the whole game. Specifically,
by letting

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\text{diff}_{1}(\bm{\mu}^{\bm{\pi}},\bm{\mu}^{\bm{\pi}^{\prime}})\coloneqq\left(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}-\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}^{\prime}}\right)^{\top}\bm{A}\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}-\tau\left(\psi_{\rm bi}^{\Pi_{1}}(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}},\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}})-\psi_{\rm bi}^{\Pi_{1}}(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}^{\prime}},\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}})\right)+\tau\left(\psi_{\rm bi}^{\Pi_{2}}(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}},\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}})-\psi_{\rm bi}^{\Pi_{2}}(\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}^{\prime}},\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}})\right)
``` |  |

and $`\text{diff}_{2}`$ similarly, then the summation of the
left-hand-side of
[Equation 4.3](#S4.E3 "In 4.2 Convergence with Full Information Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
over all infoset $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$ is equal to is equal to

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\sum_{t=1}^{T}\text{diff}_{1}(\bm{\mu}^{(\tau,\gamma),*},\bm{\mu}^{(t)})+\text{diff}_{2}(\bm{\mu}^{(\tau,\gamma),*},\bm{\mu}^{(t)})\geq 0.`$ |  | (4.4) |

The non-negativity is because $`\bm{\mu}^{(\tau,\gamma),*}`$ is the NE
of
[Equation 3.1](#S3.E1 "In 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").
By combining
([4.3](#S4.E3 "Equation 4.3 ‣ 4.2 Convergence with Full Information Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence"))
and
([4.4](#S4.E4 "Equation 4.4 ‣ 4.2 Convergence with Full Information Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")),
we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle 0\leq`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\mu^{(\tau,\gamma),*}(\sigma(s))\left(-\frac{\tau\gamma}{2}\sum_{h\in s}\mu_{c}(h)\sum_{t=2}^{T}D_{\psi_{s}^{\Delta}}(\pi_{p(s)}^{(\tau,\gamma),*}(\cdot{\,|\,}s),\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\pi\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t)}_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s))+O(\eta_{s}T)+O\left(\frac{1}{\eta_{s}}\right)\right)`$ |  |
|  | $`\displaystyle\overset{(a)}{=}`$ | $`\displaystyle-\frac{\tau\gamma}{2}\min_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\sum_{h\in s}\mu_{c}(h)\sum_{t=2}^{T}D_{\psi^{\Pi}}(\bm{\mu}^{(\tau,\gamma),*},\bm{\mu}^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\pi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t)}})+O(\max_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\eta_{s}T)+O\left(\frac{1}{\min_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\eta_{s}}\right).`$ |  |

$`(a)`$ is by Lemma
[4.1](#S4.Thmtheorem1 "Lemma 4.1 (Lemma D.2. in ( ) ). ‣ 4.2 Convergence with Full Information Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
in the following. By rearranging the terms, we can get an upperbound on
$`\sum_{t=2}^{T}D_{\psi^{\Pi}}(\bm{\mu}^{(\tau,\gamma),*},\bm{\mu}^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\pi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t)}})`$.

###### Lemma 4.1 (Lemma D.2. in [Liu et al. (2022)](#bib.bib33)).

For any strategy
$`\bm{\mu}^{\bm{\pi}},\bm{\mu}^{\widetilde{\bm{\pi}}}\in\Pi`$ and
regularizer
$`\psi^{\Delta}_{s}\colon\Delta^{|\mathcal{A}_{s}|}\to\mathbb{R}`$ for
each infoset $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$, we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle D_{\psi^{\Pi}}(\bm{\mu}^{\bm{\pi}},\bm{\mu}^{\widetilde{\bm{\pi}}})=\sum_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\mu^{\bm{\pi}}(\sigma(s))D_{\psi_{s}^{\Delta}}(\pi_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s),\widetilde{\pi}_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s)).`$ |  | (4.5) |

For completeness, the proof of the lemma can be found in
[Section E.1](#A5.SS1 "E.1 Proof of Lemma ‣ Appendix E Proof of Theorem ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").
Here is the full theorem.

###### Theorem 4.2 (Informal).

Consider the update rule
([4.2](#S4.E2 "Equation 4.2 ‣ 4.2 Convergence with Full Information Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence"))
and $`q^{(t)}(s,\cdot)`$ is chosen to be counterfactual value,
trajectory Q-value, or Q-value. When
$`\frac{\eta_{s}^{\rm anc}}{\eta_{s}}\leq\tau C_{s}^{\eta}`$, where
$`C_{s}^{\eta}`$ is a game-dependent constant, and $`\eta_{s}`$ is
smaller than a game dependent constant (formally defined in
[Appendix E](#A5 "Appendix E Proof of Theorem ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence"))
for any $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$, we have the following guarantee.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\sum_{t=2}^{T}D_{\psi^{\Pi}}(\bm{\mu}^{(\tau,\gamma),*},\bm{\mu}^{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5mu\bm{\pi}\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu^{(t)}})\leq O\left(\max_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\eta_{s}T\right)+O\left(\frac{1}{\min_{s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\eta_{s}}\right).`$ |  | (4.6) |

The proof and the formal version are postponed to Appendix
[E](#A5 "Appendix E Proof of Theorem ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").
Therefore, by choosing
$`\eta_{s}=\Theta\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\right)`$ for any
$`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$ as in
[Theorem 4.2](#S4.Thmtheorem2 "Theorem 4.2 (Informal). ‣ 4.2 Convergence with Full Information Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence"),
QFR enjoys best-iterate convergence with full-information feedback.

### 4.3 Convergence with Stochastic Feedback

We complement the results of Section
[4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Convergence with Full Information Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
by showing that the best-iterate convergence guaranteed by QFR is still
guaranteed when only visiting a trajectory at each iteration. The proof
utilizes standard concentration inequalities, incurring an additional
sublinear cost caused by the noise incurred from sampling, as recalled
in the following lemma.

###### Lemma 4.3 (Generalization of Proposition 1 in [Farina et al. (2020)](#bib.bib13)).

Let $`M,\widetilde{M}`$ be positive constants such that
$`\left|f^{(t)}(\bm{u})-f^{(t)}(\bm{u}^{\prime})\right|\leq M`$ and
$`\left|\widetilde{f}^{(t)}(\bm{u})-\widetilde{f}^{(t)}(\bm{u}^{\prime})\right|\leq\widetilde{M}`$
for any $`\bm{u},\bm{u}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}`$ for any $`t\in[T]`$,
where $`\mathcal{C}`$ is a convex set. Then, if for any $`\bm{u}`$,
$`\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{f}^{(t)}(\bm{u}){\,|\,}\widetilde{f}^{(1)},\widetilde{f}^{(2)},\cdots,\widetilde{f}^{(t-1)}]=f^{(t)}(\bm{u})`$
and $`\bm{u}^{(t)}`$ is deterministically influenced by
$`\widetilde{f}^{(1)},\widetilde{f}^{(2)},\cdots,\widetilde{f}^{(t-1)}`$,
then for any $`\delta\in(0,1)`$ and $`\bm{u}\in\mathcal{C}`$, we have

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\Pr\Bigg(\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(f^{(t)}(\bm{u})-f^{(t)}(\bm{u}^{(t)})\right)\leq\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\widetilde{f}^{(t)}(\bm{u})-\widetilde{f}^{(t)}(\bm{u}^{(t)})\right)+(M+\widetilde{M})\sqrt{2T\log\frac{1}{\delta}}\Bigg)\geq 1-\delta.`$ |  |

Next, we will substitute the following values into
[Lemma 4.3](#S4.Thmtheorem3 "Lemma 4.3 (Generalization of Proposition 1 in ( ) ). ‣ 4.3 Convergence with Stochastic Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence"),

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle f^{(t)}_{s}(\bm{u})\coloneqq\frac{1}{\mu_{p(s)}^{(t)}(\sigma(s))}\langle q^{(t)}(s,\cdot),\bm{u}\rangle-\tau\mu_{-p(s)}^{(t)}(s)\psi_{s}^{\Delta}(\bm{u})`$ |  |
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\widetilde{f}^{(t)}_{s}(\bm{u})\coloneqq\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\mu_{p(s)}^{(t)}(\sigma(s))}\langle\widetilde{q}^{(t)}(s,\cdot),\bm{u}\rangle-\frac{\tau}{\mu_{p(s)}^{(t)}(\sigma(s))}\psi_{s}^{\Delta}(\bm{u})&s{\text{ is visited at timestep }t}\\
0&\text{Otherwise}\end{cases}`$ |  |

, where $`\widetilde{q}^{(t)}`$ is defined in
[Algorithm 1](#alg1 "In 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").
The proof of $`\widetilde{f}^{(t)}_{s}`$ being an unbiased estimator of
$`f^{(t)}_{s}`$ is postponed to
[Appendix F](#A6 "Appendix F Proof of Theorem ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").
Then,
[Equation 4.3](#S4.E3 "In 4.2 Convergence with Full Information Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
can be bounded by
$`\sum_{t=1}^{T}\left(\widetilde{f}^{(t)}_{s}(\pi_{p(s)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s))-\widetilde{f}^{(t)}_{s}(\pi_{p(s)}^{(t)}(\cdot{\,|\,}s))\right)`$,
which can be further bounded by analyzing the update-rule
[Equation 3.2](#S3.E2 "In 14 ‣ Algorithm 1 ‣ 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").
The analysis is similar to the one in
[Section 4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Convergence with Full Information Feedback ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
and can be found in
[Appendix F](#A6 "Appendix F Proof of Theorem ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").
Finally, we have
[Theorem 3.1](#S3.Thmtheorem1 "Theorem 3.1 (Informal). ‣ 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").

## 5 Experiments

In the experiments, we apply QFR in 4-Sided Liar’s Dice, Leduc Poker
([Southey et al., 2005](#bib.bib45)), Kuhn Poker ([Kuhn,
1950](#bib.bib27)), and $`2\times 2`$ Abrupt Dark Hex. The learning rate
is the *same* in all infosets, unlike what the theorem requires, which
shows that QFR is easier to implement than what the theory suggests.
Note that for MMD ([Sokota et al., 2023](#bib.bib44)), there is no
theory for convergence when using trajectory Q-value and Q-value as
feedback, while QFR has.

In order to pick hyperparameters, we performed a grid-search for QFR and
MMD on learning rate $`\eta`$, regularization $`\tau`$, perturbation
$`\gamma`$, and the regularizer is either negative entropy or Euclidean
distance. For BalancedOMD (BOMD) ([Bai et al., 2022](#bib.bib2)) and
BalancedFTRL (BFTRL) ([Fiegel et al., 2023](#bib.bib16)), we applied
grid search to the learning rate $`\eta`$ and fixed the exploration rate
(IX parameter) to $`\frac{\eta}{20}`$ as suggested in [Fiegel et al.
(2023)](#bib.bib16). For the outcome-sampling CFR / CFR+, we also
applies grid-search on the exploration parameter. The details of
experiments, the comparison between QFR and baselines with
full-information, and the ablation study of QFR when the strategy is
approximated by neural network, can be found in Appendix
[H](#A8 "Appendix H Experiment Details ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").

Figure 1: Exploitability of
[Algorithm 1](#alg1 "In 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
in 4 benchmark games. We can see that QFR outperforms outcome-sampling
CFR / CFR+, MMD, and BOMD in all games. It outperforms BFTRL in all
games except Liar’s Dice. For each line, we repeat the experiments 100
times with different seeds.​​

The experimental result of
[Algorithm 1](#alg1 "In 3 Q-Function based Regret Minimization (QFR) ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
is presented in
[Figure 1](#S5.F1 "In 5 Experiments ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").
In the experiments, QFR and MMD are both using an unbiased estimator of
the trajectory Q-value, while CFR and CFR+ use an unbiased estimator of
the counterfactual value.

Figure
[1](#S5.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 5 Experiments ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
shows that QFR outperforms outcome-sampling CFR, CFR+, and BOMD in all
games. Moreover, QFR outperforms BFTRL in all games except Liar’s Dice,
with a relatively small gap (QFR 0.174 v.s. BFTRL 0.167 in
exploitability). The reason may be Liar’s Dice is too easy since it can
be solved within 50 iterations with full-information feedback (see
[Figure 2](#A8.F2 "In Appendix H Experiment Details ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
in
[Appendix H](#A8 "Appendix H Experiment Details ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")).
Lastly, QFR outperforms MMD in all games.

The superiority of QFR over CFR, CFR+, BOMD, and BFTRL may be attributed
to both the additional regularization (best-iterate convergence) and the
avoidance of importance sampling. For MMD, QFR is superior due to the
optimistic updates, since optimistic updates allow predictions of the
gradients at the next iteration. The code of QFR and baselines for
tabular games can be found in
[LiteEFG](https://github.com/liumy2010/LiteEFG/tree/main/LiteEFG/baselines)³³
3 https://github.com/liumy2010/LiteEFG/tree/main/LiteEFG/baselines ([Liu
et al., 2024](#bib.bib32)).

## 6 Conclusions and Future Work

In this paper, we focused on the question of whether the following
properties can coexist while solving two-player zero-sum extensive-form
games: (I) learning with random rollouts, (II) converging in iterates,
and (III) avoiding importance sampling. These properties are standard
desiderata in the reinforcement learning literature for single-agent
settings, but have thus far eluded extensive-form games. The answer is
affirmative, and we propose an algorithm, QFR, that achieves all of
them. We hope this work can serve as a step in the direction of bridging
the gap between reinforcement learning techniques and
imperfect-information extensive-form games. In the future, it would be
interesting to investigate the performance of algorithms that are both
sound and work well in large domains.

Supplementary Material

 

## Appendix A Additional Related Work

This section compares our paper to prior literature on three aspects:
convergence guarantees, notion of values used by the algorithm, and
support of value estimation via rollouts. We provide a visual comparison
of the most relevant algorithms in
[Table 1](#S1.T1 "In 1 Introduction ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence").

Convergence Guarantee. Most CFR-based algorithms \[[Zinkevich et al.,
2007](#bib.bib51), [Tammelin et al., 2015](#bib.bib48), [Steinberger
et al., 2020](#bib.bib47)\] only guarantee that the *average* strategy
converges to an NE, though empirically some variants of CFR exhibit
last-iterate convergence \[[Bowling et al., 2015](#bib.bib4), [Tammelin
et al., 2015](#bib.bib48)\]. Motivated by the success of *Optimistic
Mirror Descent* (OMD) achieving last-iterate convergence in normal-form
games (NFGs) \[[Mertikopoulos et al., 2019](#bib.bib35), [Wei et al.,
2021](#bib.bib50), [Cai et al., 2022](#bib.bib7)\], [Farina et al.
\[2019b\]](#bib.bib12) first empirically showed that OMD also enjoys
last-iterate convergence in EFGs. Then, [Lee et al.
\[2021\]](#bib.bib30) theoretically proved that *Optimistic
Multiplicative Weights Update* (OMWU), an instance of OMD, converges in
EFGs with unique NE assumption.

Use of Q-Values. To achieve last-iterate convergence in EFGs, additional
regularization and optimism are widely used. [Perolat et al.
\[2021\]](#bib.bib38) used that approach in continuous time, using
counterfactual values under full-information (*i.e.*, non-sampled)
feedback. [Lee et al. \[2021\]](#bib.bib30), [Liu et al.
\[2023\]](#bib.bib31) achieved last-iterate convergence in EFGs using
discrete-time updates, but both of their convergence results are based
on counterfactual values in the non-sampled case. [Sokota et al.
\[2023\]](#bib.bib44)’s MMD algorithm empirically observed convergence
by using sampled (trajectory) Q-values in conjunction with entropic
regularization, without theoretical guarantees. In this paper, we
combine regularization and optimism, and obtain a theoretically sound
algorithm (QFR) for solving two-player zero-sum EFGs using sampled
Q-values / trajectory Q-values.

Rollout-based estimation. [Lanctot et al. \[2009\]](#bib.bib28) proposed
Outcome-Sampling Monte-Carlo CFR (OS-MCCFR), a variant of CFR which uses
random rollouts to estimate counterfactual values. Later, [Farina and
Sandholm \[2021\]](#bib.bib10), [Farina et al. \[2021b\]](#bib.bib15),
[Bai et al. \[2022\]](#bib.bib2), and [Fiegel et al.
\[2023\]](#bib.bib16) proposed algorithms that converge in EFGs with
trajectories at each iteration. However, those algorithms rely on
importance sampling, which causes numerical instability due to the large
range of feedback. ESCHER [McAleer et al. \[2023\]](#bib.bib34) and
LocalOMD \[[Fiegel et al., 2024](#bib.bib17)\] sample trajectories
off-policy. This is usually undesirable as it favors exploring parts of
the game tree according to uniform random probability, rather than
focusing on those that are more likely given the policy.
ARMAC \[[Gruslys et al., 2020](#bib.bib19)\] and ACH \[[Fu et al.,
2021](#bib.bib18)\] support both Q-values and approximately-on-policy
estimation, but like ESCHER and LocalOMD they do not guarantee
convergence of the iterates. Moreover, neither of them is
computationally efficient since they need to sample many trajectories
(possibly infinite) at each iteration to ensure that the estimation of
feedback is totally accurate. Also, ACH does not converge to the set of
NEs, even in terms of average-iterate convergence.

## Appendix B Formal Definition of Trajectory Q-Value, Q-Value, and Counterfactual Value

In the following, we will formally define trajectory Q-value, Q-value,
and counterfactual value respectively.

For any $`s\in{\mathcal{S}},a\in\mathcal{A}_{s}`$, the *trajectory
Q-value*
$`\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(s,a)`$
is formally defined as,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(s,a)\coloneqq\frac{1}{\pi_{1}(a{\,|\,}s)}\sum_{h^{\prime}\colon\exists h\in s,(h,a)\sqsubseteq h^{\prime}}\mu_{c}(h^{\prime})\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}(\sigma_{1}(h^{\prime}))\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}(\sigma_{2}(h^{\prime}))\mathcal{U}_{1}(h^{\prime})`$ |  | (B.1) |

In
([B.1](#A2.E1 "Equation B.1 ‣ Appendix B Formal Definition of Trajectory Q-Value, Q-Value, and Counterfactual Value ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")),
$`\mu_{c}(h)`$ denotes the probability of reaching $`h`$ contributed by
the chance player. We use
$`\left\{\mathcal{U}_{i}\colon\mathcal{H}\to[-1,1]\right\}_{i\in[2]}`$
to denote the utility assigned to each player at each node of the game.
We assume this is nonzero only at terminal nodes (nodes with an empty
action set) without loss of generality.

At the same time, we can define the *Q-value*, defined as the expected
utility *conditioned on* reaching infoset $`s`$,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle Q_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(s,a)\coloneqq\frac{\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(s,a)}{\sum_{h\in s}\mu_{c}(h)\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}(\sigma_{1}(h))\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}(\sigma_{2}(h))}.`$ |  | (B.2) |

Typically, algorithms like CFR and its variants \[[Tammelin et al.,
2015](#bib.bib48), [Brown and Sandholm, 2019](#bib.bib5)\] use
counterfactual values as feedback to learn the equilibrium. The
counterfactual value at infoset $`s`$ of player $`i`$ is the Q-value at
$`s`$ times the reach probability of the opponent and the chance player
to $`s`$. Formally, for any $`s\in{\mathcal{S}},a\in\mathcal{A}_{s}`$,
we can define the *counterfactual value*
$`\mathsf{CF}_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(s)`$ as,

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{CF}_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(s,a)\coloneqq\sum_{h^{\prime}\colon\exists h\in s,(h,a)\sqsubseteq h^{\prime}}\mu_{c}(h^{\prime})\frac{\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}(\sigma_{1}(h^{\prime}))}{\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}(s,a)}\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}(\sigma_{2}(h^{\prime}))\mathcal{U}_{1}(h^{\prime}).`$ |  | (B.3) |

$`\mathsf{CF}_{i}^{\bm{\pi}}`$ is not compatible with rolling out
trajectories as reinforcement learning usually does, since a rolling
trajectory includes the probability of both players reaching infoset
$`s`$, which hinders extending algorithms to large games.

Also, for any $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}`$ and $`a\in\mathcal{A}_{s}`$
(similar for player $`2`$), we have

|  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{CF}_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(s,a)=`$ | $`\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}(\sigma(s))}\mkern 1.5mu\overline{\mkern-1.5muQ\mkern-1.5mu}\mkern 1.5mu_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(s,a)`$ |  |  | (B.4) |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathsf{CF}_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(s,a)=`$ | $`\displaystyle\frac{\sum_{h\in s}\mu_{c}(h)\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}(\sigma_{1}(h))\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}(\sigma_{2}(h))}{\mu_{1}^{\pi_{1}}(\sigma(s))}\cdot Q_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(s,a)\overset{(i)}{=}`$ | $`\displaystyle\sum_{h\in s}\mu_{c}(h)\mu_{2}^{\pi_{2}}(\sigma_{2}(h))Q_{1}^{\bm{\pi}}(s,a),`$ |  | (B.5) |

where equality $`(i)`$ follows from $`\sigma_{1}(h)=\sigma(s)`$ for any
$`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}`$ and $`h\in s`$, which is a consequence to the
perfect-recall assumption.

## Appendix C Stability of Trajectory Q-value and Q-Value

In this section, we will show the stability of trajectory Q-value and
Q-value, *i.e.* proving Property
[2](#Thmproperty2 "Property 2 (Stability). ‣ 4.1 Preliminaries and Basic Properties ‣ 4 Analysis ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
when the regularizer in each infoset $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$ can be
written as,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle\psi_{s}^{\Delta}(\bm{u})=\begin{cases}\frac{\alpha_{s}}{2}\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{s}}u_{a}^{2}&(\text{Euclidean Norm})\\
\alpha_{s}\left(\log|\mathcal{A}_{s}|+\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}_{s}}u_{a}\log u_{a}\right)&(\text{Negative Entropy}),\end{cases}`$ |  | (C.1) |

where $`\alpha_{s}>0`$ is a state-dependent constant. We add
$`\log|\mathcal{A}_{s}|`$ to the negative entropy to ensure the
regularizer is always positive. Previous work \[[Kroer et al.,
2020](#bib.bib26)\] chose specific $`\alpha_{s}`$ to ensure the dilated
regularizer associated with $`\psi_{s}^{\Delta}`$ is 1-strongly convex.
For generality of the result, we keep the $`\alpha_{s}`$ in the
regularizer.

Due to the symmetry between two players, we will only prove that for
$`s\in{\mathcal{S}}_{1}`$. Moreover, to stabilize trajectory Q-value and
Q-value, the learning rate need to satisfy the following conditions.

1.  (A)
    $`\max_{h\in s}\sum_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\sqsubseteq\sigma_{i}(h)}\eta_{s^{\prime}}\leq\eta_{s}`$
    for any $`s\in{\mathcal{S}},i\in[2]`$
2.  (B)
    $`\allowbreak 6\eta_{s}^{\rm anc}\max_{s^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{S}}}\allowbreak\left(\frac{2\left\|\bm{q}\right\|_{\infty}}{\alpha_{s^{\prime}}}+\frac{\tau}{M_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\gamma}\right)\leq 1`$
    for any $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$, where
    $`\left\|\bm{q}\right\|_{\infty}\coloneqq\max_{t\in[T],s\in{\mathcal{S}}}\left\|q^{(t)}(s,\cdot)\right\|_{\infty}`$
    and its upperbound is given in
    [Lemma G.1](#A7.Thmtheorem1 "Lemma G.1 (Upperbound of Feedback 𝑞^(𝑡)⁢(𝑠,⋅)). ‣ G.1 Upperbound of Feedback ‣ Appendix G Auxiliary Lemmas ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
3.  (C)
    $`\eta_{s}\left(2\left\|\bm{q}\right\|_{\infty}+\frac{\tau\alpha_{s}}{M_{1}}\log\frac{1}{\gamma}\right)\leq 1`$
    for any $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$

[A](#A3.I1.i1 "Condition A ‣ Appendix C Stability of Trajectory Q-value and Q-Value ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
ensures that
$`\sum_{(s^{\prime},a^{\prime})\sqsubseteq\sigma(s)}\eta_{s^{\prime}}\leq 2\eta_{s}^{\rm anc}`$
for any $`s\in{\mathcal{S}}`$.
[B](#A3.I1.i2 "Condition B ‣ Appendix C Stability of Trajectory Q-value and Q-Value ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence"),
[C](#A3.I1.i3 "Condition C ‣ Appendix C Stability of Trajectory Q-value and Q-Value ‣ A Policy-Gradient Approach to Solving Imperfect-Information Games with Best-Iterate Convergence")
